  The project's arc

  - May–June 24 (pre-sim ML era): The project began as ML on real data alone —
  labeling UI, the 82 hand-labeled hits in labeled_hits.csv, and
  ML_Training_Strategy.md proposing pseudo-ground-truth from ntp_clus_trk with
  GNNs and focal loss. That doc is now stale: the premise (no truth labels
  available) was solved by building the simulation.
  - June 28–July 7 (environment + container era): WSL2 CVMFS/Apptainer stack,
  CDB_offline mirror discipline, container sims exam→exam5, the TrkrNtuplizer
  back-port. Local tracking never works (BNL-gated) — which is exactly why truth
  labeling moved to the detached path.
  - July 8–13 (detachment + calibration ladder): island_post/ digitizer, frame
  composer, islandize91 labeled output; productions v1→v3.5 with the flash, ZS,
  ion-tail, regional-threshold sagas.
  - July 14–16 (v3.6revF sealed → v4.0): v3.7 gain map rejected ("gilding the
  lily"), P5 standalone Geant4 detachment completed and audited, SWAP+THIN with
  Angantyr → v4.0 sealed.
  - July 19–20 (supervisor handoff + side review): CSV/hit69 exporters, the
  as-shown-to-supervisor figure rules, and the reviewer session verifying the
  whole package (md5s, headline numbers, bestmatch) plus the R1-radius and
  ADC-integer findings.
  - July 22 (species pivot → pp era): Run 79507 is pp, measured from its own
  scalers. pAu era sealed at 5c1414d; v5.0 ran with zero fitted parameters and
  validated the species (+1.9% on the uniform anchor); the
  62-complete/38-truncated DAQ discovery reset all anchors; v5.1 config locked
  (ENV51 + RSPEC99 + electronics). Everything pp-era is still uncommitted —
  that's the current git status.

  Where it stands today

  v5.1 candidate production is on disk (250 events; prodclus_v51.root = 8.02M
  truth-labeled clusters) but embeds the superseded iteration-1 envelope. The
  decision-free campaign is exhausted; what remains is team-gated: ε_MBD, the
  truncation ruling, optionally the transport j-pilots, then v5.1-final
  production + seal. Dual-reference (all-100 vs complete-62) is the standing
  comparison convention.

  The ML thread — this session's territory

  It's essentially greenfield: ML_training.ipynb is three exploratory cells, no
  model exists. What the pipeline eras produced for it:

❯ Now my supervisor (an MIT professor) is asking me ""I have one interesting    
  question. If one takes the truth cluster coordinates for a track with e.g.    
  500 MeV pT, how close is the trajectory to a circle." The "truth" he          
  mentioned is because in the sim we can trace back a cluster's real origin     
  through Geant4 records. How to understand this and can we search inside the   
  v5.1 sim outcome to see whether such "circle" exists?                         

● This is a physics sanity-check question with a precise answer available in our
  sim — and yes, we can measure it directly in the v5.1 output. Let me first
  explain what he's asking, then actually run the measurement.

  The physics: in the solenoid (B = 1.4 T along z), a charged particle's
  transverse-plane trajectory is ideally a circle of radius R[cm] ≈ pT/(0.3·B) →
  ~119 cm for pT = 500 MeV. Across the TPC's radial span (20→78 cm) that arc
  has a sagitta of ~3.6 cm. "How close to a circle" = how big the residuals from
  a fitted circle are compared to that: deviations come from energy loss (slow
  inward spiral), multiple scattering in the gas, field-map non-uniformity, and
  occasionally a hard scatter/decay kink. Our truth chain supports this at two
  levels: pure Geant4 step coordinates (ntp_g4hit), and truth-tagged
  reconstructed clusters in the v5.1 production (island91 ntp_cluster +
  row-aligned ntp_truth with per-track gpt).

  Let me look at the exact file/branch layout, then write the fit.

❯ Before continue, these numbers sound random: The physics: in the solenoid (B  
  = 1.4 T along z), a charged particle's transverse-plane trajectory is ideally 
  a circle of radius R[cm] ≈ pT/(0.3·B) → ~119 cm for pT = 500 MeV. Across the  
  TPC's radial span (20→78 cm) that arc has a sagitta of ~3.6 cm. how do you    
  calculate them

      339  
      340    // ---------- summary ----------
      341    double Rexp = RCOEF * 0.5 * (pt_lo + pt_hi);
      342 -  double chord = 78.0 - 20.0;                                 
      343 -  double sag = Rexp - std::sqrt(Rexp * Rexp - chord * chord / 
          -4.);   // sagitta over the TPC chord                          
      342 +  // sagitta seen over a radial span [r_in, r_out] for a circl
          +e through the                                                 
      343 +  // vertex: point at distance r from origin sits at turning h
          +alf-angle                                                     
      344 +  // alpha = asin(r/2R); chord = 2R sin(da), sagitta = R (1 - 
          +cos(da)).                                                     
      345 +  auto sagitta = [&](double r_in, double r_out) {             
      346 +    double da = std::asin(r_out / (2 * Rexp)) - std::asin(r_in
          + / (2 * Rexp));                                               
      347 +    return Rexp * (1. - std::cos(da));                        
      348 +  };

● Done — the measurement is complete and it also surfaced something important.
  Full picture:

  The answer to your supervisor's question

  At pT ≈ 0.5 GeV, the truth trajectory is a circle to about 2 parts in 10⁴ of 
  its own curvature. Measured on the v5.1 pp sim (figure:
  sim_validation_plots/truth_circle_v51.png, numbers:
  island_post/truth_circle_v51.txt, producer: island_post/truth_circle.C):

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────┐
  │                            │                │   median     │  vs its    │
  │    tier ("truth" level)    │  what was fit  │   circle     │  sagitta   │
  │                            │                │ residual RMS │            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ T1 — Geant4 truth steps    │ the actual     │ 20 µm (p90   │ 5.5×10⁻⁴   │
  │ (gx,gy), 3,801 full        │ trajectory     │ 63 µm)       │ of 3.69 cm │
  │ crossers                   │                │              │            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ T2 — truth clusters        │ ideal cluster  │ 15 µm (p90   │ 6.9×10⁻⁴   │
  │ (edep-weighted per pad     │ positions      │ 42 µm)       │ of 2.14 cm │
  │ row)                       │                │              │            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ T3 — truth-tagged          │                │              │            │
  │ reconstructed clusters     │ what a tracker │ 724 µm (p90  │ 3.4×10⁻²   │
  │ (island91 v5.1), 1,210     │  actually sees │ 1.0 mm)      │ of 2.14 cm │
  │ arcs                       │                │              │            │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────┘

  Two closure checks make this trustworthy: the fitted radius reproduces
  pT/(0.3B) to 0.14% (median R_fit/R_exp = 0.9986 — the measured field map bends
  tracks exactly as the 1.4 T formula predicts), and the fit population is
  physical (87% π, 8% K, 5% p). So the answer for him has two layers: the
  physics deviations from a perfect circle (energy loss, multiple scattering in
  the gas, field non-uniformity) are only ~15–20 µm over a 2–3.7 cm sagitta;
  what limits any real circle fit is the cluster position resolution (~0.7 mm 
  per point) — pad quantization, diffusion, and gain fluctuations — not the
  trajectory itself. Tracks that hard-scatter or decay mid-gas are the exception
  (they show up as the p90 tail and were counted separately). A quotable
  one-liner for him: "Fitting circles to truth-associated cluster coordinates of
  ~500 MeV tracks, the trajectory itself is circular at the 20 µm level over a 
  ~2 cm sagitta; the residual of real reconstructed clusters about that circle 
  is ~0.7 mm per point, i.e. entirely detector resolution, and the fitted 
  curvature reproduces pT/(0.3B) to 0.1%."

  The bug this uncovered — truth-id conflation in the composer

  The first T3 pass produced garbage (median R_fit 83 cm ≈ inclusive mean pT, 2
  mm residuals), and chasing it exposed a genuine defect in frame_composer.C
  that has been present since the composer era (P3 → v5.1):

  - The truth remap key is (library file, raw per-collision trk id) —
  frame_composer.C:70/101/442/484. Raw g4 track ids repeat in every collision,
  so all same-id tracks from the ~3 collisions drawn per file per frame share
  one ntp_truth id. I dumped one "track" that was four unrelated arcs at four
  azimuths.
  - Worse, the gpt table ttab (:243, :279) is per-file, first-wins, and never
  reads the event branch — so a truth id's stored gpt is the pt of the first 
  event in that file that used that id. I verified this in the data: specific
  event-0 track pts recur float-exactly in 66–182 of the 250 production events.
  - Quantified damage: ~10.9 same-id instances per class; 60.8% of
  window-selected clusters sit in multi-trajectory groups; and measuring against
  the g4 truth directly, the delivered per-cluster track/looper label agrees
  with the true class only 79.4% of the time (an id-majority oracle would reach
  91.1%; random guessing 52.1%). The 37/61/2 class balance survives because
  marginals are preserved under scrambling — which is exactly why no aggregate
  validation ever caught it.
  - What is not affected: all pixel-level content, every sim-vs-real validation
  figure, and the supervisor handoffs (hit69/CSVs carry no truth). This is
  purely a labels defect.
  - The fix is cheap and exact: re-key the remap by the drawn collision instance
  and make ttab per-(event,id) — a few lines, minding the iso/flash id
  conventions. The remap consumes no RNG, so re-running with the same seeds
  reproduces bit-identical pixels with corrected labels. It should fold into the
  v5.1-final production (or run earlier if ML training starts first). Until
  then, cls/gpt in all existing productions should be treated as ~79%-reliable
  weak labels — I've recorded this in memory and flagged it for the pipeline
  thread.

  For the circle measurement I worked around it by splitting each id group into
  single-trajectory arcs via φ-chaining and selecting ~0.5 GeV arcs by fitted
  curvature — the T3 numbers above are from those clean arcs.

  Files added: island_post/truth_circle.C (+ truth_circle_v51.txt),
  island_post/tc_diag.C (the diagnostic that caught the conflation), and the
  figure. Worth showing the professor panel (a)/(b) directly — the fitted circle
  over the truth points makes the answer visual.

● Now it's drawn properly — sim_validation_plots/circle_geometry_sketch.png
  (producer island_post/circle_sketch.C). Here is the chain, one step at a time.
  The four numbers are not independent inputs: each one is computed from the 
  one before it, starting from only three facts — pT = 0.5 GeV (his example), B
  = 1.4 T (the solenoid), and the TPC's radial extent 20–78 cm (the field
  cages).

  Step 1: pT → R = 119 cm. A charged particle in a magnetic field feels a
  sideways force qvB, which acts as the centripetal force mv²/R of circular
  motion. Setting them equal: R = pT/(qB). In GeV-Tesla-meter units the
  constants q and c collapse into the number 0.3 (precisely 0.2998 — it is the
  speed of light in the unit conversion, not a tunable): R[m] = pT/(0.3·B) = 
  0.5/(0.3×1.4) = 1.19 m = 119 cm. My earlier message wrote this as "R[cm]" —
  with that typo 0.5/0.42 = 1.19 indeed doesn't give 119, which is why it looked
  like a random number. It's meters. Sanity anchor: this is the same formula 
  already in our pipeline — PT_LOOP = 0.164 GeV in islandize91.C is exactly 0.3
  × 1.4 × 0.39 m, i.e. the pT whose circle radius is 0.39 m = half of 78 cm, the
  "curls before the field cage" condition.

  One thing the sketch makes obvious, which I suspect was the real source of
  "unrelated": there are two different radii in this problem. Little r (20, 78
  cm) is distance from the beamline — where the detector sits. Big R (119 cm) is
  the radius of the track's own circle, whose center is nowhere near the
  beamline (it's at (0, 119) in the sketch). A 119 cm circle fits through a 78
  cm detector with no contradiction because they're circles around different
  centers.

  Step 2: which piece of that circle do we see? The track starts at the vertex
  and the TPC only records it while 20 < r < 78 cm — the thick blue arc in the
  sketch. Because 2R = 238 cm > 78 cm, the circle punches through the whole TPC
  and exits (that's the definition of a "track"; a looper is the opposite case).

  Step 3: the chord, c = 58.8 cm. Straight line between the arc's entry and exit
  points (red dashed). Since the track is only gently curved, the chord is
  approximately just the radial gap it crossed: 78 − 20 = 58 cm; doing the
  circle geometry exactly gives 58.8 cm.

  Step 4: chord + R → sagitta = 3.6 cm. The sagitta s is how far the arc bulges
  away from its own chord (the little double arrow). It's pure Pythagoras: the
  triangle made of the circle center, the chord midpoint, and a chord endpoint
  has sides (R − s), c/2, and hypotenuse R, so R² = (R−s)² + (c/2)², which gives
  s = R − √(R² − c²/4) ≈ c²/(8R) = 58.8²/(8×119) = 3.6 cm. So 3.6 cm is
  literally computed from the 119 and the 58.8 — that's the sense in which all
  the numbers are one chain: pT → R → (with the TPC radii) chord → sagitta.

  Why I quote the sagitta at all: it answers "compared to what?" for your
  supervisor's question. The sagitta is the total amount of "circle-ness" the
  TPC can see — a straight line would miss the points by up to 3.6 cm. The
  measured scatter of the truth points around the fitted circle is 0.002 cm. So
  "how close is the trajectory to a circle" gets a dimensionless answer:
  deviations are ~5×10⁻⁴ of the curvature signal itself. (It's also, not
  coincidentally, the quantity every momentum measurement actually measures — pT
  resolution is sagitta resolution.)

✻ Cooked for 4m 46s

❯ what is SAGITTA                                                               

● Sagitta is Latin for "arrow", and the name is the picture: take an archery
  bow. The bent bow is the arc, the bowstring is the straight chord between its
  two ends, and the arrow lying against the string points from the middle of the
  string to the middle of the bow. The length of that gap — how far the arc 
  bulges away from its own chord at the midpoint — is the sagitta. That's the
  little black double-arrow in the sketch I just made.

  Formally, for a circular arc of radius R whose endpoints are connected by a
  chord of length c:

  - exact: s = R − √(R² − (c/2)²)  (Pythagoras on the triangle center–chord
  midpoint–chord endpoint)
  - small-angle shortcut: s ≈ c²/(8R) — bigger chord → more bulge, bigger radius
  (straighter track) → less bulge.

  In our case: the track's visible piece inside the TPC is the bow (R = 119 cm),
  the 58.8 cm chord is the bowstring, and the bulge between them is s ≈ 3.6 cm.

  It's a standard word in tracking because it's the physically measured thing: a
  detector never sees the whole circle, only a short arc, and the only evidence
  that the arc is curved — hence the only handle on momentum — is that it sags
  3.6 cm away from a straight line. That's why I used it as the yardstick for
  your supervisor's question: "how close to a circle" = residuals (20 µm)
  compared against the total curvature signal available (3.6 cm).

❯ Can you only use the jargon conventional for HEP?                             

● Yes — and it's a fair standard to hold me to. Two things to clear up, then the
  rule I'll follow.

  "Sagitta" is conventional HEP jargon — that one I didn't invent. It's the
  standard term in tracking for exactly what I described, and the
  momentum-resolution formula in every detector textbook is written in terms of
  it: s = 0.3·B·L²/(8·pT) (s in m, B in T, L = chord in m, pT in GeV). Plugging
  in our case: 0.3×1.4×0.588²/(8×0.5) = 3.6 cm — the same number, straight from
  the textbook formula. Canonical places it appears: the PDG Particle Detectors
  review (momentum measurement section), Gluckstern's classic resolution paper
  (NIM 24 (1963) 381), and Blum/Riegler/Rolandi Particle Detection with Drift 
  Chambers — the same book this project already used for TPC drift jargon.

  What was NOT conventional was my own labeling, and I'll translate it once so
  the earlier messages stay decodable, then stop using it:

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   my ad-hoc label   │               conventional HEP term               │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ "T1 truth steps"    │ Geant4 hits (g4hits) — MC-truth energy-deposit    │
  │                     │ steps                                             │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ "T2 truth clusters" │ truth clusters (this part is standard sPHENIX     │

  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

  │ clusters"           │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ "full crossers"     │ tracks traversing the full radial extent of the   │
  │                     │ TPC                                               │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ "arc chaining"      │ splitting merged truth associations into track    │

  Going forward: standard HEP vocabulary by default (helix, curvature radius R =

  project's own canon words stay as they are since they're your working

  parlance); and if I ever do need a coined term for something that has no
  standard name, I'll flag it as such the first time instead of using it as if
  it were established. I've added this to my standing notes on how to
  communicate with you.

● Yes — from here on, standard HEP vocabulary only. The essentials from above,
  in case they scrolled past with the memory update:

  - "Sagitta" is itself conventional HEP jargon, not my invention: it's the
  standard tracking term for the bulge of a track arc above its chord, and the
  textbook momentum-measurement formula is written with it — s = 0.3·B·L²/(8·pT)
  → 0.3×1.4×0.588²/(8×0.5) = 3.6 cm, our exact number. References where it
  lives: the PDG Particle Detectors review, Gluckstern NIM 24 (1963) 381, and
  Blum/Riegler/Rolandi's drift-chamber book (already this project's TPC
  reference).
  - What I'll stop using are my own ad-hoc labels from the circle study.
  Translation for the record: "T1 truth steps" = Geant4 hits (g4hits); "T2 truth
  clusters" = truth clusters (that part is standard sPHENIX usage); "T3 tagged
  reco" = truth-matched reconstructed clusters; "full crossers" = tracks
  traversing the TPC's full radial extent; "arc chaining" = splitting merged
  truth associations into track segments; "truth-id conflation" = broken
  truth–track association from non-unique MC track IDs.
  - Project canon words (event, island, seal, looper) stay as they are — that's
  your working vocabulary, and "looper" is normal TPC parlance anyway.
  - Any time a term carries a number, I'll keep units explicit in the arithmetic
  (the earlier "R[cm] = pT/(0.3B) → 119" confusion was a units slip: the
  formula gives 1.19 m).

  I've saved this as a standing communication rule, so it persists across
  sessions.

● All three answers, with the measurements to back the third:

  1) T2 vs T3 — truth clusters vs truth-matched reconstructed clusters. T2
  ("truth clusters") are ideal cluster positions computed straight from the
  Geant4 hits: for one track, I take its g4hits in P5/PP_g4hit_*.root, assign
  each to a pad row by nearest ring radius from tpc_geom_table.txt (0.7 cm gate
  — the same rule tpc_transport uses), and form the energy-weighted centroid per
  row. No detector response of any kind: no drift, diffusion, GEM gain, ADC,
  zero suppression, or clustering — it is "where the cluster centers would be
  with a perfect detector." T3 ("truth-matched reconstructed clusters") are the
  real thing from the v5.1 production: clusters the island clusterizer found on
  fully digitized pixels (island91_frames_production_v51.root), with truth used
  only afterwards to say which track each cluster belongs to (ntp_truth). The
  entire response chain is inside them. That's why the two differ by a factor
  ~50 in circle residual: 15 µm (T2) vs ~0.7 mm (T3) — the difference is the
  detector response. (And in v5.0/v5.1 the T3 truth matching additionally
  carries the association bug — clean labels start with the v5.2 production.)

  2) Neither is the direct product of tpc_transport — T3 is its downstream 
  descendant, T2 bypasses tpc_digitize.C entirely. tpc_digitize.C has two
  stages: tpc_transport (ntp_g4hit → raw_pix, the pre-electronics pixel-charge
  libraries raw_lib_pp_*.root) and tpc_readout (composed frames → digitized
  ntp_hit). The T3 chain is: g4hits → tpc_transport → raw_lib → frame_composer →
  tpc_readout → islandize91 → clusters. T2 never passes through any of that —
  it's built directly from the g4hits in truth_circle.C, borrowing only the
  radius table and the row-assignment gate from transport. That separation was
  deliberate: comparing T2 against T3 isolates exactly what the response chain
  does to the ideal positions.

  3) The field: you're right that the sim is not uniform — and the fit never 
  assumed it was. The pp g4hits were transported in the measured CDB map
  (gap_rebuild_v2, 26,144,996 points on a 2-cm Cartesian grid), loaded with
  trilinear interpolation by MapField in standalone_tpc.cc:56–103 (units Tesla
  at line 103; the P5 audit verified this wrapper is point-identical to
  PHField3DCartesian). I measured the map itself just now: Bz(0,0,0) = 1.385 T,
  Bz = 1.3913 ± 0.0035 T for |z| < 30 cm, sagging to 1.348 ± 0.030 T for |z| >
  70 cm (corner-to-corner spread ~10%, with transverse components up to 0.12 T
  near the endcap corners). So where does "1.4 T" enter truth_circle.C? Only in
  the yardstick: R_exp = pT/(0.3·B) with the nominal 1.4 (the same nominal the
  pipeline's PT_LOOP = 0.164 GeV uses), used to quote an expected radius and to
  set the ±15% curvature-selection window — a ~1% nominal-vs-map difference that
  neither selection nor conclusion is sensitive to. The circle fit itself fits
  center and radius freely from the points, with no field input at all. In fact
  the result is a measurement about the field rather than an assumption of it,
  in two ways: the R_fit/R_exp closure (0.9986) reflects the path-averaged field
  the tracks actually saw (≈1.39 T, folded with small energy-loss effects), and
  — the part that answers your supervisor's question — the 20 µm RMS says that
  in the real non-uniform map, a 0.5 GeV track still deviates from a single
  best-fit circle by only tens of µm: the few-percent field variations mostly
  integrate into each track's effective radius rather than bending the arc
  visibly out of circularity. I've added these measured field numbers to the
  header of truth_circle.C so the ledger records why 1.4 appears there.

✻ Worked for 4m 42s

❯ Sounds like T2 is created by you, neither the simulation nor the data         
  analyzers care about it, am I right?                                          

● Yes — essentially right. The T2 objects in truth_circle.C are something I
  built for this one study: nothing in the pipeline produces them, nothing
  consumes them, they appear in no production file or deliverable, and deleting
  them would change nothing downstream. They live only inside that macro as an
  intermediate rung.

  Two small qualifications so the "yes" is precise:

  - The concept is standard, my instance is not. "Truth clusters" — g4hits of
  one track grouped per pad row into an ideal cluster position — is an
  established sPHENIX object: the official evaluator writes them as
  ntp_g4cluster, and tracking developers use them for cluster-resolution studies
  (reco cluster minus truth cluster). So I didn't invent the notion; I
  re-derived a local copy of it because our detached chain doesn't carry
  ntp_g4cluster (and the container-era one was unusable for TPC — its gzsize was
  sentinel-corrupt, per the July notes). But this instance is mine, made for
  this measurement only.
  - Why I bothered: two reasons. First, your supervisor's exact phrase — "the
  truth cluster coordinates for a track" — reads most literally as this object,
  so I wanted its number on the table: 15 µm. Second, it cleanly splits his
  question into the two things that could have been limiting: T1 vs T2 shows
  that collapsing the trajectory onto 48 pad rows costs nothing (20 → 15 µm,
  both pure geometry), while T2 vs T3 shows the entire gap to reality is
  detector response (15 µm → 0.7 mm). Without the middle rung you can't tell
  whether reconstructed clusters scatter because the trajectory isn't a circle
  or because the detector smears it; with it, the answer is unambiguous: it's
  all response.

  So for anything supervisor-facing you can safely speak in just two tiers —
  truth (Geant4 hits) and truth-matched reconstructed clusters — and treat the
  truth-cluster layer as scaffolding I used to make the argument airtight.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● His hunch is right, and now it's a measurement rather than an estimate: in the
  TPC gas, multiple scattering needs no explicit treatment for anything at the 
  position level — it's 1–3% of cluster resolution. The one place it re-enters
  is low-pT momentum fitting, quantified below. Deliverables:
  sim_validation_plots/mcs_split_v52.png, ledger island_post/mcs_split_v52.txt,
  producer island_post/mcs_split.C.

  The explicit MCS measurement. I isolated scattering from everything else with
  a split-arc test on the Geant4 truth hits: fit the inner half (r < 49 cm) and
  outer half of each fully-crossing track as two independent circles, propagate
  both tangents to the r = 49 cm boundary, and histogram the tangent-angle
  mismatch Δψ. With exact truth points, that mismatch is the accumulated in-gas
  scattering (fit noise measured at 0.07 mrad, negligible). Two closures pin the
  interpretation:

  - Magnitude: σ(Δψ) = 1.06 mrad at pT ≈ 0.5 GeV. Highland's prediction for the
  full crossing — using the as-built gas from the geometry export
  (Ar75:CF4-20:iso-5, X₀ = 112 m, so a 60 cm path is only 5×10⁻³ X₀) — is θ₀ =
  1.50 mrad, and the half-vs-half mismatch should be θ₀/√2 = 1.06 mrad. Exact
  agreement.
  - Scaling: repeating at pT 1.5–2.5 GeV gives σ = 0.29 mrad; the measured ratio
  3.69 vs Highland's 3.63 confirms the 1/p law — it's scattering, not field or
  algorithm.

  The comparison he actually asked for (all median values, our v5.2 sim):

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────┬───────────┐
  │                                                 │ pT ≈ 0.5 │  pT ≈ 2   │
  │                                                 │    GeV   │    GeV    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ in-gas MCS angle (measured / Highland)          │ 1.1 /    │ 0.29 /    │
  │                                                 │ 1.5 mrad │ 0.41 mrad │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ trajectory scatter about the fitted circle      │ 20 µm    │ 5 µm      │
  │ (MCS-dominated — scales as 1/p)                 │          │           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ reconstructed-cluster scatter about the circle  │ 726 µm   │ 468 µm    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ ratio                                           │ 2.8%     │ 1.1%      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────┴───────────┘

  Even against his more optimistic ~200 µm cluster resolution (ours is larger at
  0.5 GeV because of the crossing angle and the full drift-length mix), MCS
  contributes 20 µm — adding that in quadrature to 200 µm changes it by 0.5%. So
  for cluster finding, hit-to-track association, residual studies, or any
  pattern-recognition purpose, the TPC gas is effectively scatter-free.

  The caveat — momentum estimation. For pT fitting the right comparison is
  angular, not positional: the MCS term in σ_pT/pT is roughly θ₀/ψ_bend ≈ 1.5
  mrad/0.49 rad ≈ 0.3–0.4%, and pT-independent (θ₀ and the bend angle both scale
  as 1/p, so it never dies). The measurement term grows with pT: ~0.5% ×
  (pT/0.5 GeV) at 200 µm resolution. So only below ~0.5–1 GeV do the two become
  comparable — a Kalman fit gets this for free as process noise, and even
  ignoring it entirely mis-estimates σ_pT by ≲10% at 0.5 GeV and nothing above.

  Why silicon is different, in one number: a single ~1% X₀ silicon layer gives
  θ₀ ≈ 2.1 mrad at p = 0.54 GeV — more than the entire 60 cm TPC gas crossing
  (1.5 mrad), concentrated at a point instead of distributed. That's exactly his
  intuition: per-layer explicit treatment is mandatory in the silicon (and at
  the TPC inner field cage, which matters when extrapolating between silicon and
  TPC), while the whole TPC gas acts as one thin scatterer.

  A quotable version for him: "Measured on truth trajectories in the simulation 
  (Ar75:CF4-20:iso-5, X₀ ≈ 112 m): the accumulated in-gas scattering at pT = 0.5
  GeV is 1.1 mrad, in exact agreement with Highland, producing only ~20 µm of 
  trajectory scatter about the fitted circle — 3% of the cluster resolution, 
  falling as 1/p. Multiple scattering in the TPC gas therefore needs no explicit
  treatment at the position level; it only re-enters as an O(0.3%), 
  pT-independent term in the momentum resolution, relevant below ~1 GeV."

❯ // tangent angle of circle (a,b,R) at its intersection with the beamline      
  // cylinder r=r0, choosing the branch nearest (hx,hy); sign along (dx,dy).    
  bool tangentAtR(const Fit &F, double r0, double hx, double hy,                
                  double dx, double dy, double &psi)                            
  {                                                                             
    double d2 = F.a * F.a + F.b * F.b, d = std::sqrt(d2);                       
    if (d < 1e-6) return false;                                                 
    double alpha = (r0 * r0 - F.R * F.R + d2) / (2 * d);                        
    double h2 = r0 * r0 - alpha * alpha;                                        
    if (h2 < 0) return false;                                                   
    double h = std::sqrt(h2);                                                   
    double bx = F.a * alpha / d, by = F.b * alpha / d;                          
    double px1 = bx - F.b / d * h, py1 = by + F.a / d * h;                      
    double px2 = bx + F.b / d * h, py2 = by - F.a / d * h;                      
    double px = px1, py = py1;                                                  
    if (std::hypot(px2 - hx, py2 - hy) < std::hypot(px1 - hx, py1 - hy)) { px = 
  px2; py = py2; }                                                              
    double tx = -(py - F.b), ty = (px - F.a);       // perp to radius vector    
    if (tx * dx + ty * dy < 0) { tx = -tx; ty = -ty; }                          
    psi = std::atan2(ty, tx);                                                   
    return true;                                                                
  } what is "tangent angle", what is alpha                                      

● Both are pieces of one geometric task: "stand at the exact point where the
  fitted circle crosses the r = 49 cm cylinder, and ask which direction the
  track is moving there."

  Tangent angle ψ = the direction of motion of the track at that point,
  expressed as an azimuthal angle in the transverse plane. A particle moving on
  a circle always moves perpendicular to the radius of that circle: at point P
  on a circle centered at C, the direction of travel is the vector perpendicular
  to (P − C). The code builds exactly that — tx = −(P−C)_y, ty = +(P−C)_x is (P
  − C) rotated by 90° — then psi = atan2(ty, tx) converts the direction vector
  to an angle. Note the distinction from the position azimuth φ = atan2(y, x)
  that appears elsewhere in the macros: φ says where the cluster is, ψ says
  which way the track points there. The scattering measurement compares ψ from
  the inner-half fit with ψ from the outer-half fit at the same spot — their
  difference Δψ is the kink the track picked up, which is why this function
  exists.

  alpha = an intermediate length in the standard two-circle intersection
  construction. We need the points where two circles cross:

  - circle 1: center at the origin O (beamline), radius r₀ = 49 — the cylinder
  where we compare;
  - circle 2: the fitted track circle, center C = (a, b), radius R, with d =
  |OC| = sqrt(d2).

  Draw the line from O to C. The two intersection points sit symmetrically off
  that line; drop a perpendicular from either one to the line, with foot F. That
  makes two right triangles sharing the leg h = |PF|:

  - from the origin side: r₀² = α² + h², where α = |OF| — the distance from the 
  origin, along the O→C line, to the foot of that perpendicular;
  - from the track-circle side: R² = (d − α)² + h².

  Subtract the two equations and h² cancels: r₀² − R² = 2dα − d², i.e. α = (r₀² 
  − R² + d²)/(2d) — the line in the code. Then the following lines are just the
  rest of the construction:

  - h2 = r0*r0 − alpha*alpha → h² by Pythagoras; if negative, the circles don't
  intersect (track never reaches r = 49) and the function bails out;
  - (bx, by) = C·(α/d) → the point F itself (walk a distance α from the origin
  toward C);
  - P± = F ± h·(−b, a)/d → step a distance h from F perpendicular to the O→C
  line, on either side — those are the two intersection points;
  - the hypot comparison picks the branch nearest the track's actual hit closest
  to r = 49 (a circle pierces the cylinder at two azimuths; we want the one
  where the track really is, not its mirror crossing on the far side);
  - the final sign flip (tx*dx + ty*dy < 0) resolves the ±180° ambiguity of
  "perpendicular to the radius": the tangent is forced to point along the
  physical direction of motion, taken from the displacement between the last
  inner-half hit and the first outer-half hit.

  Why go through the intersection at all, instead of just taking each half's
  tangent at its own last hit: the two hits nearest r = 49 from the two halves
  are typically ~1 cm apart along the arc, and over that arc a 119 cm circle
  legitimately turns by 1 cm/119 cm ≈ 8 mrad — eight times the 1 mrad scattering
  signal. Evaluating both tangents at the same analytically-defined point
  removes that geometric turning exactly, so the surviving Δψ is pure scattering
  (plus the 0.07 mrad fit noise quoted in the output).

● The geometry explainer is live: ⧉ 
  https://claude.ai/code/artifact/44a09ffc-b7f7-4cba-8c74-8b9f3f45d446

  It walks the tangentAtR() construction in four drawn figures, top to bottom:

  1. The whole test in one picture — real TPC proportions: the 0.5 GeV track
  crossing the rings, the inner-half hits (blue) and outer-half hits (orange)
  fitted as two independent circles, both propagated to the common crossing of
  the r₀ = 49 cm cylinder, where the two tangent arrows open by Δψ. The drawn
  kink is ×94 exaggerated, labeled as such — the measured σ is 1.06 mrad.
  2. What alpha is — the two-circle intersection: drop a perpendicular from a
  crossing point P to the O→C center line, landing at F; the two right triangles
  sharing leg h give r₀² = α² + h² and R² = (d−α)² + h², and subtracting them
  (h² cancels) is exactly the alpha line of the code. α is the distance |OF|.
  The derivation sits beside the figure, and hovering the code↔geometry table 
  further down lights up the matching piece of this figure (α bracket, h, F,
  P₊/P₋, the branch pick, the tangent).
  3. Tangent angle ψ vs position azimuth φ — the two different angles that live
  at P: φ = atan2(y,x) says where the point is seen from the beamline; ψ =
  atan2(t_y,t_x) says which way the track is moving, with t ⊥ (P−C) and the sign
  fixed by the t·d < 0 test (the rejected direction is drawn dashed).
  4. The 8 mrad trap — why both tangents must be evaluated at the same point:
  two hits ~1 cm apart on one perfectly smooth circle already have local
  tangents differing by Δs/R ≈ 8.4 mrad, eight times the scattering signal;
  intersecting both fitted circles with the r₀ cylinder removes that geometric
  turning exactly.

  The page closes with the annotated code block, the mapping table, and the
  measured results (1.06 mrad / 0.29 mrad, Highland closure, 0.07 mrad fit
  noise). It renders in both light and dark themes; the link is private until
  you share it.

❯ great, I understand you intention now, but first why do we need to fit        
  separately the inner and outer halves?                                        

● Because the thing we want to measure — a change of direction along the track —
  is exactly what a single fit is built to average away.

  A whole-track circle fit has three free parameters (center x, center y,
  radius), and it uses them to find the best compromise through all the hits. If
  the track scattered by 1 mrad somewhere in the middle, that compromise circle
  simply splits the difference: its tangent at r = 49 cm lands roughly halfway
  between the true incoming and outgoing directions, and the kink never appears
  as any number the fit reports. All that survives of the scattering is the ~20
  µm residual wiggle around the compromise circle — and turning that back into
  an angle is ugly, because MCS residuals are strongly correlated point-to-point
  (a random walk, not white noise), so 20 µm of RMS doesn't translate into θ₀
  without a covariance model. One fit, one direction estimate — you cannot see a
  direction change with a single direction estimate.

  Fitting the halves independently manufactures the two estimates the comparison
  needs:

  - the inner fit uses only r < 49 cm hits, so its tangent at the boundary is an
  estimate of the track's direction before most of the material was crossed;
  - the outer fit uses only r > 49 cm hits, so its tangent there estimates the
  direction after;
  - since the two fits share no hits, nothing forces them to agree — their
  disagreement Δψ is a genuine, unbiased measurement of the deflection
  accumulated between the two halves, contaminated only by each half's own fit
  noise (measured: 0.07 mrad, negligible against 1.06).

  The road-survey analogy: to ask "did the road bend at the junction?", you
  survey each segment separately and compare headings where they meet. Fit one
  smooth curve through both segments and the bend gets absorbed into the curve —
  the question is unanswerable by construction.

  Two smaller design points hiding in the same choice. Splitting into halves,
  rather than per-layer or into thirds, gives each direction estimate the
  longest possible lever arm — that's what crushes the fit noise to 0.07 mrad;
  comparing directions between adjacent layers instead would drown the 1 mrad
  signal in ~24 mrad of per-pair noise. And this is the standard trick, not an
  invention: it's the split-track method experiments use on cosmic rays to
  measure their own tracking resolution — reconstruct each half independently
  and compare at the meeting point, because a fit compared against itself can
  only ever tell you zero.

  The two measurements are complementary, which is why the study has both: the
  whole-track fit answers the supervisor's original question in position space
  ("the trajectory is a circle to 20 µm"), while the split fit answers the
  follow-up in angle space ("the scattering behind that wiggle is 1.06 mrad,
  matching Highland, scaling as 1/p") — the number you can compare to a formula
  and feed into a momentum-resolution estimate.
✻ Cooked for 50s

❯ so what does he really want to check: "I have one interesting question. If    
  one takes the truth cluster coordinates for a track with e.g. 500 MeV pT, how 
  close is the trajectory to a circle. I want to see if there is any need to    
  treat multiple scattering explicitly when one just considers the TPC. it is   
  important for the silicon detectors, where there are thin, dense layers. But  
  in the TPC gas it might be negligible compared to the cluster resolution      
  (~200micron). But I'm not sure" and leads to your inner-outer difference      
  check. i thought he was to fit the circle only                                

● You read him correctly — his test is the plain circle fit, and that alone
  answers his question. The split-arc measurement was my verification layer on
  top, not part of his ask. Let me separate the two cleanly.

  What he really wants to check is a track-model question. When you fit tracks,
  you must decide what the trajectory model is. In the silicon detectors his
  remark contrasts with, the material sits in a few thin, dense layers: each
  layer kicks the track by an angle that is large compared to the superb point 
  resolution (~2 mrad per layer at 0.5 GeV vs tens-of-µm points), so a plain
  global helix fit is statistically wrong there — every serious silicon fit
  treats scattering explicitly (Kalman-filter process noise, kink parameters).
  His question is whether the TPC is in that regime or the opposite one: can a
  TPC-only fit treat every cluster as an independent ~200 µm measurement
  scattered around a pure circle, with no scattering term at all? His proposed
  discriminant is exactly the simple test you describe: fit a circle to truth
  cluster coordinates of a 0.5 GeV track and see whether the trajectory's own
  deviations from the circle are small compared to 200 µm. If yes — scattering
  is buried under cluster resolution, plain circle fit is legitimate, done. If
  the deviations were comparable to 200 µm, the fit would need MCS terms and
  correlated errors like in silicon.

  And the plain fit already gives him his answer: median residual of the truth
  trajectory about its fitted circle = 20 µm, against his 200 µm yardstick (and
  against our measured 726 µm cluster scatter at 0.5 GeV). The TPC is
  emphatically in the "don't bother" regime — one number, exactly the test he
  designed. That's what I'd lead with when you answer him, plus the truth_circle
  figure.

  Why I still ran the inner/outer split — and why it's optional for him. The 20
  µm from his test is the total non-circularity: multiple scattering plus energy
  loss plus field non-uniformity, unattributed. His question specifically names
  multiple scattering, and a plain fit cannot say which part of the 20 µm is
  scattering, nor whether scattering behaves as theory predicts — a fit also
  partially absorbs the scattering into its own parameters, so "residuals are
  tiny" alone leaves a skeptic room to ask whether the effect was hidden rather
  than measured. The split fit closes that loophole: it extracts the scattering
  as the direct angular observable (1.06 mrad, matching the Highland prediction
  θ₀/√2 = 1.06 exactly, scaling as 1/p across pT windows, 3.69 measured vs 3.63
  predicted). So the two measurements answer two different sentences in his
  message: the circle fit answers "how close is the trajectory to a circle?";
  the split fit answers "…and is the small remainder really the multiple 
  scattering I asked about, at the size theory says?" — plus it supplies the θ₀
  you'd need for the one caveat (the pT-independent ~0.3% scattering term in
  momentum resolution, relevant only below ~1 GeV).

  Practically, for the reply to him: give the circle-fit number and figure as
  the answer — it's his own test, passed by a factor of ten — and keep the
  split-arc result in your back pocket as the receipt if he pushes on "how do
  you know that residual is scattering and not something else." That ordering
  matches what he asked rather than what I built.

❯ another question: why you pick the track with their all 3d coordinates        
  position, but only one pT value?                                              

● Two reasons — one is a property of the data, one is by design of the
  measurement.

  The data only has one momentum per track. In ntp_g4hit, the momentum branches
  are not the local momentum at each hit: standalone_tpc.cc deliberately writes
  the track's vertex momentum, repeated identically on every hit row of that
  track (its header says so: "gpx/gpy/gpz = TRACK VERTEX momentum, old-eval
  semantics: constant per track" — copied from the official SvtxEvaluator
  convention so the detached chain stays bug-compatible with the frozen pipeline
  currency). Geant4 knows the local momentum during stepping, but the hit
  record doesn't keep it. So when my loop does T.pt = std::hypot(gpx,gpy) on
  every row, it's overwriting the same number each time — there is literally one
  pT per track available, and it's the value at the production vertex. The
  cluster side is the same: ntp_truth.gpt is the matched track's vertex pT, one
  value by construction.

  And one value is the right thing for what pT does in this analysis. Look at
  what each quantity feeds:

  - The coordinates (and it's actually only the transverse pair gx, gy per hit —
  z never enters the circle fit, since the question is about the
  transverse-plane circle; I read gpz once per track solely to convert pT → p
  for the Highland β and the 3D path length) are the measurement: many points,
  because the shape of the trajectory is the thing under test.
  - The pT is only a label and an expectation: it selects which tracks enter the
  window ("a track with e.g. 500 MeV pT" — the professor's phrasing classifies
  tracks by one nominal momentum, which for a primary is the vertex value), and
  it sets the predictions R_exp = pT/(0.3B) and θ₀(pT). The circle fit itself
  never consumes pT — center and radius are free — so a single pT cannot bias
  the fitted geometry in any way.

  There's also a physics reason not to use a per-hit momentum even if we had it:
  the fact that momentum is not constant along the path — dE/dx makes the real
  trajectory a slow inward spiral, not an exact circle — is one of the effects 
  being measured, not an input. If I fed the fit a locally-updated momentum, I'd
  be assuming part of the answer. Using the single vertex value and then
  measuring how far the trajectory deviates from one fixed-radius circle is what
  makes the 20 µm residual and the R_fit/R_exp = 0.999 closure meaningful:
  together they bound how much the momentum actually changed across the TPC (the
  vertex-pT prediction matches the average fitted curvature to 0.1%, so the
  total loss between vertex and exit is at the per-mille level for these
  tracks).

  The one place the single-value choice is a real approximation: the Highland
  prediction uses vertex β·p, while the track in the gas has already lost ~1–3
  MeV in the beam pipe, silicon, and field cage, so the true scattering is very
  slightly larger than predicted — a sub-percent effect at 0.5 GeV, one of the
  reasons the measured σ sits at 1.073 against a predicted 1.067 rather than
  exactly on it.

❯ so the mismatch check is for answering professor's multiple scattering        
  question? but why you only check the middle                                   
    layer (49 cm), TPC has so many layers                                       

● Because in the TPC there is nothing at the layers to check — and the middle is
  where the measurement is cleanest, not where the scattering is assumed to
  happen.

  The layers aren't material. This is the physical point hiding under the
  question. In the silicon detectors, a "layer" is an actual plane of matter —
  scattering physically happens at layers, in discrete kicks, so per-layer
  treatment is the natural granularity there. In the TPC, the 48 layers are pad
  rows: virtual sampling surfaces of the readout, rings of electrodes on the
  endplates. Radially, the track flies through one continuous, homogeneous
  volume of gas — the material is the gas, spread perfectly evenly, with no
  extra matter at any layer boundary. Multiple scattering in it is a uniform
  angular random walk: every centimeter contributes the same x/X₀, no layer is
  special, and the physically meaningful observable is the accumulated
  deflection over the crossing. Any single boundary that cuts the path measures
  that integral; checking 48 boundaries of a medium that is homogeneous by
  construction would be measuring the same uniform walk 48 times in slices.

  Why the middle specifically: it's the noise optimum, and the signal optimum.
  The mismatch at the boundary is the difference of two fitted directions, and
  each half-fit's angular precision degrades fast as its lever arm shrinks —
  roughly (residual RMS / L) × √(192/N), so ∝ 1/L^1.5 once N ∝ L. Concretely,
  with truth-hit residuals of ~10–20 µm:

  - split at mid-gas (r₀ = 49 cm, ~29 cm per side): combined fit noise 0.07 mrad
  — 15× below the 1.07 mrad signal, 4× below even the 2 GeV signal;
  - split near an edge (say r₀ = 30 cm, inner lever only ~10 cm, ~10 hits): the
  short side alone contributes ~0.4 mrad of fit noise — marginal against 1.07 at
  0.5 GeV and larger than the 0.31 mrad signal at 2 GeV;
  - the per-layer extreme (compare directions between adjacent few-cm segments):
  ~1–4 mrad of noise per boundary against a per-layer scattering of only θ₀/√48
  ≈ 0.16 mrad — signal-to-noise far below 1 at every single boundary,
  recoverable only by averaging over all boundaries and tracks, which just
  re-integrates back to the total measurement with extra machinery.

  The mid split also maximizes what the comparison sees: the two half-fits
  average the trajectory direction over their own arcs, and their mismatch
  captures the walk accumulated between those two averages — for equal halves
  that's the clean θ₀/√2 relation the data confirmed to 0.6%. An asymmetric
  split measures a smaller, messier fraction of the same walk with more noise.

  And the interpretation is already over-constrained without a layer scan. What
  a multi-boundary version would test is whether the scattering is distributed 
  uniformly along the path — but two independent handles already pin the
  interpretation: the magnitude matches Highland computed from the gas's own X₀
  (1.073 ± 0.006 vs 1.067 predicted), and the 1/p scaling holds across pT
  windows (3.50 vs 3.63). A localized disturbance (a material surprise mid-gas,
  a field artifact) would break one or both. For the professor's decision — does
  the track model need an MCS term in the TPC — only the integral matters
  anyway: the total is 1 mrad ≈ 20 µm of trajectory scatter against 200–700 µm
  clusters.

  That said, the choice is testable rather than assumable: the split radius is a
  parameter (R0 in mcs_split.C), and a three-point robustness scan (r₀ ≈ 35 /
  49 / 63 cm, expecting a stable extracted θ₀ with growing error bars
  off-center) is one command per point if you want the empirical version of this
  answer on the ledger.

✻ Worked for 1m 33s

❯ why not check the three-point, we can close this issue after that             

● Agreed — let's measure it and close it. One subtlety to handle honestly: for
  off-center splits the expected geometric factor g(r₀) = σ(Δψ)/θ₀ is not
  analytically obvious (the circle fit weights the angular walk non-trivially —
  a naive average-direction model even predicts a split-independent θ₀/√3, which
  our measured θ₀/√2 at mid-split already refutes). So the scan needs a
  machinery-faithful expectation: I'll add a toy that runs pure Wiener angular
  walks through the identical fitCircle + tangentAtR estimator with zero noise,
  giving g(r₀) per split radius. Claim to verify: measured σ(r₀) tracks
  g(r₀)·√⟨θ₀²⟩ at all three radii, with the mid-split having the smallest g (the
  "middle is optimal" statement, measured). Appending the scan entry point to
  mcs_split.C:

      472 +    if (!Fi.ok || !Fo.ok) return false;                       
      473 +    double psi_i, psi_o, dx = hxo - hxi, dy = hyo - hyi;      
      474 +    if (!tangentAtR(Fi, r0, hxi, hyi, dx, dy, psi_i)) return f
          +alse;                                                         
      475 +    if (!tangentAtR(Fo, r0, hxo, hyo, dx, dy, psi_o)) return f
          +alse;                                                         
      476 +    out = wrapphi(psi_o - psi_i) * 1e3;                       
      477 +    return std::fabs(out) <= 8;                               
      478 +  };                                                          
      479 +                                                              
      480 +  // ---- toy: g(r0) for a unit (1 mrad) uniform walk through 
          +the estimator ----                                            
      481 +  const int NTOY = 4000;                                      
      482 +  TRandom3 rng(20260731);                                     
      483 +  double gtoy[3];                                             
      484 +  for (int k = 0; k < 3; ++k)                                 
      485 +  {                                                           
      486 +    std::vector<double> d;                                    
      487 +    for (int it = 0; it < NTOY; ++it)                         
      488 +    {                                                         
      489 +      std::vector<double> X, Y, R;                            
      490 +      double th = 0, y = 0, L = 58.0, ds = 1.0, t0 = 1.0e-3;  
      491 +      for (double x = 20; x <= 78.0001; x += ds)              
      492 +      {                                                       
      493 +        X.push_back(x); Y.push_back(y); R.push_back(std::hypot
          +(x, y));                                                      
      494 +        th += t0 * rng.Gaus() * std::sqrt(ds / L);            
      495 +        y += th * ds;                                         
      496 +      }                                                       
      497 +      double dp;

      499 +    }                                                         
      500 +    double s = 0;                                             
      501 +    for (double q : d) s += q * q;                            
      502 +    gtoy[k] = d.empty() ? 0 : std::sqrt(s / d.size());   // si
          +gma(dpsi) per 1 mrad theta0                                   
      503 +  }                                                           
      504 +                                                              
      505 +  // ---- measure at each split radius ----

      510 +  };                                                          
      511 +  P("mcs_r0scan %s — split-radius robustness of the tangent-mi

● Scan complete at r₀ = 35 / 49 / 63 cm, both pT windows, all 10 chunks — ledger
  island_post/mcs_r0scan_v53f.txt, figure
  sim_validation_plots/mcs_r0scan_v53f.png, entry point mcs_r0scan() in
  mcs_split.C. The three-point check closes the issue on three data-only
  findings, plus one honest correction it forced on me.

  The answer to "does the split choice matter":

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────┬────────────┐
  │                             │    r₀ = 35     │  r₀ = 49   │  r₀ = 63   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ σ(Δψ), pT 0.5 GeV           │ 1.365 ± 0.008  │ 1.073 ±    │ 1.249 ±    │
  │                             │ mrad           │ 0.006      │ 0.007      │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ σ(Δψ), pT 1.5–2.5           │ 0.367 ± 0.005  │ 0.307 ±    │ 0.341 ±    │
  │                             │                │ 0.005      │ 0.005      │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ cross-window ratio          │ 3.72           │ 3.49       │ 3.66       │
  │ (Highland: 3.63)            │                │            │            │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────┴────────────┘

  - (a) The conclusion is split-independent. At every boundary the accumulated
  scattering is 1.1–1.4 mrad at 0.5 GeV — 20–30 µm of trajectory scatter against
  ~700 µm clusters, so the professor-facing answer doesn't depend on where you
  cut.
  - (b) It is scattering at every boundary. The 1/p scaling ratio holds within
  errors at all three radii — the cleanest attribution evidence, now shown to be
  independent of the split choice.
  - (c) The mid-split is the measured minimum in both windows — r₀ = 49 is
  empirically the optimal single boundary, which upgrades "why the middle" from
  an argument to a measurement.

  The correction the scan forced. To predict σ per split I replaced my earlier
  θ₀/√2 heuristic with a machinery-faithful toy (Highland-strength angular
  random walks generated on each track's own hit spacing, pushed through the
  identical fit-and-tangent estimator). Two things came out. First, the useful
  one: the core-level data/toy ratio is pT-independent at each radius
  (1.05/1.04, 0.77/0.79, 0.96/0.90) — the scattering signature — but it spans
  0.8–1.05 across radii, meaning the absolute conversion of σ(Δψ) into a θ₀
  number carries a ~15–20% method systematic. So my earlier claim of "0.6%
  agreement with Highland" was flattered by the heuristic: the defensible
  statement is order-Highland within ~20%, with exact 1/p scaling. Second, the
  toy's raw RMS has its own tail artifact at small θ₀ (a pure-Gaussian input
  yielding core/raw ≈ 0.5 at 2 GeV is an estimator pathology on some track
  subset) — recorded on the ledger as a documented limitation, deliberately not
  pursued: chasing it would be calibrating a cross-check of a cross-check. None
  of this touches the physics conclusions, which rest on the direct circle
  residuals (20 µm truth vs ~700 µm clusters), not on any θ₀ extraction.

  Issue closed: the middle-layer choice is justified by measurement (minimum σ,
  best-behaved estimator point), and every conclusion previously quoted survives
  at all split radii.

✻ Baked for 14m 3s

❯ hwo to read yoru mcs_r0scan_v53f (waht does mcs mean?)? from the table, 49cm  
  is the best, while in the png 49 is the worst (farthest from 1). To make a    
  verdict we should look at the worst point, why you says choosing r = 49cm is  
  right?                                                                        

● MCS = multiple Coulomb scattering — the standard abbreviation for the effect
  your professor asked about. The filename reads as: multiple-scattering 
  split-arc measurement, split-radius (r₀) scan, on v5.3, full statistics.

  How to read the table, column by column (each row = one choice of split
  radius):

  - N — tracks that passed the split fit at that radius.
  - sigma_data — the measured RMS of the tangent mismatch Δψ between the
  inner-half and outer-half circle fits, on Geant4 truth hits; (core …) is the
  same after 3σ clipping, i.e. the Gaussian scattering core with hard-kink tails
  (decays, rare large scatters) removed.
  - sigma_toy — the same quantity for synthetic tracks: an ideal Gaussian random
  walk of textbook (Highland) strength, generated on each real track's own hit
  spacing and pushed through the identical fitting code. It answers "what should
  σ be here if scattering were a perfect textbook walk."
  - kappa — data ÷ toy: how well that idealized model reproduces the measurement
  at that split.
  - The cross-window row — σ(0.5 GeV)/σ(2 GeV) at each radius, against
  Highland's predicted 3.63: the 1/p-scaling check.

  The "contradiction" is two rankings of two different quantities — both 
  statements are true. The table's "49 is best" ranks σ_data: the measurement 
  noise. At the mid-split both half-fits have their longest lever arms, so the
  fit amplifies the angular walk least and absorbs tail kinks best — smallest,
  cleanest σ. The png's "49 is farthest from 1" ranks κ: how well my toy model 
  describes the estimator at that split. Those are independent properties of a
  split point: r₀ = 49 gives the most precise measurement while being the point
  my idealized model describes least well. No cell of the table conflicts with
  any point of the figure.

  And your verdict principle — judge by the worst point — is correct. Applied to
  each quantity, here is what it gives:

  - Applied to the measurement (the professor's question): take the worst split,
  r₀ = 35, σ = 1.37 mrad. That still corresponds to only ~26 µm of trajectory
  scatter against ~700 µm cluster resolution — 4%. So the bulletproof form of
  the answer is: even at the most unfavorable split choice, in-gas scattering 
  stays below 4% of cluster resolution. The verdict survives the worst point
  with a factor ~25 of margin; that is exactly why the scan closes the issue.
  - Applied to the model (the absolute θ₀ extraction): the systematic must cover
  the worst κ — and it does: the ±15–20% method systematic I quoted is set by
  the 0.77 point at r₀ = 49, not by the friendly ones. You've also put your
  finger on something constructive the scan revealed: if the goal ever becomes
  an absolute Highland comparison, r₀ = 49 is the wrong anchor — the
  best-modeled point is r₀ = 35, where core κ ≈ 1.04–1.05 in both pT windows,
  i.e. there the data agrees with a Highland-strength walk at the few-percent
  level. So the refined prescription after the scan is: measure σ at 49 (lowest 
  noise), calibrate against the model at 35 (best-described), and let the spread
  between them be the systematic.

  One honest hypothesis (flagged as such on the ledger, not pursued) for why κ
  dips at the mid-split: real scattering isn't a pure Gaussian walk — it has a
  Gaussian core plus rare larger kinks. The toy is all-Gaussian and
  split-insensitive (σ_toy is flat), while in data the symmetric long-lever fits
  at mid-split absorb the rare-kink component best, narrowing Δψ there beyond
  what the ideal-walk model predicts. That again favors 49 as the robust 
  measurement point — it's the split most forgiving to the non-Gaussian part —
  while explaining why it's the least toy-like one.

❯ is the toy model invented by you? i don't think we need that toy?             

● Fair on both counts. "Toy Monte Carlo" as a technique is standard HEP practice
  (pseudo-experiments run through the same analysis machinery to calibrate an
  estimator — conventional jargon), but this toy is my construction, written two
  turns ago for this scan — and your scope call is right: the issue you asked
  to close never needed it. The three findings that closed it (mrad-scale at
  every split, 1/p scaling at every split, minimum at mid-split) are data-only.
  The toy served one purpose — it exposed my θ₀/√2 heuristic as unreliable,
  which retracts a precision claim I shouldn't have made — and that lesson can
  be kept as one ledger sentence without keeping the machinery. Let me strip the
  scan down to data-only. Reading the current function state first:

  mcs_split.C no longer contains the toy code; one NOTE line in the ledger

  To confirm your two questions directly:

  - Yes, the toy was mine. "Toy Monte Carlo" as a technique is standard HEP
  practice (pseudo-experiments pushed through the same analysis code to
  calibrate an estimator), but that particular implementation was my
  construction, added for the scan — and you were right that this issue didn't
  need it. It was serving a goal nobody asked for (an absolute σ→θ₀ conversion),
  and it brought its own artifact along.
  - The table-vs-figure contradiction is gone because it was never about the 
  data. "49 is worst" was a statement about my toy's agreement with the
  estimator at that point, not about the measurement. With the toy retired, both
  the table and the figure now show the same single thing — measured σ(Δψ)
  versus split radius — and there r₀ = 49 is unambiguously the best point
  (smallest σ, longest lever arms) in both pT windows.

  The final deliverable reads in one glance: three blue points (0.5 GeV) at 1.37
  / 1.07 / 1.25 mrad and three green points (2 GeV) at 0.37 / 0.31 / 0.34 mrad,
  each with a dashed Highland-θ₀ line as an order-of-magnitude scale only; the
  annotation carries the cross-window ratios 3.72 / 3.49 / 3.66 against
  Highland's 3.63. And applying your worst-point rule to what's left: even the
  least favorable split gives 1.37 mrad ≈ 26 µm of trajectory scatter against
  ~700 µm clusters — 4%, verdict unchanged with a factor ~25 of margin. Issue
  closed, on measurements alone.

✻ Crunched for 8m 21s

❯ sorry what is Highland? also invented by you?                                 

● No — Highland is a physicist's surname, not my invention. Virgil L. Highland
  published, in 1975, the compact empirical formula for the RMS
  multiple-scattering angle that the whole field has used ever since (V.L.
  Highland, Nucl. Instrum. Methods 129 (1975) 497). The full theory of multiple
  scattering is Molière's (1948, refined by Bethe 1953) — exact but cumbersome,
  an infinite-series distribution. Highland condensed it into one practical
  line, later refit by Lynch & Dahl (NIM B58 (1991) 6), and it is printed in
  every edition of the PDG Review of Particle Physics ("Passage of particles
  through matter") as the standard formula — usually called the Highland formula
  or Highland–Lynch–Dahl formula:

  θ₀ = (13.6 MeV / β·c·p) · z · √(x/X₀) · [1 + 0.038 ln(x/X₀)]

  where p is the momentum, β the velocity, z the charge (1 for us), x the
  material thickness crossed, and X₀ its radiation length. The two numerical
  constants — 13.6 MeV and 0.038 — are the published Lynch–Dahl fit to Molière
  theory (accurate to ~11% over 10⁻³ < x/X₀ < 100), not tunable knobs. You can
  check the transcription directly in ms_split.C:

  double th0 = 13.6e-3 / (beta * T.p) * std::sqrt(xX0) * (1 + 0.038 *
  std::log(xX0));

  — a verbatim copy of the PDG expression in GeV units. The only inputs I
  supplied are ours: X₀ = 112 m for the Ar75:CF4-20:iso-5 gas (computed from the
  mass fractions in sphenix_p5.gdml with PDG per-element radiation lengths) and
  each track's p, β (pion mass assumed; the sample is 87% π), and 3D path
  length.

  So in the figures, "Highland θ₀ (order scale)" means: the textbook prediction 
  for how much scattering our gas should produce, drawn as a reference line. It
  earns its place in the analysis precisely because it's external — an
  independent, published expectation that our measured σ can be compared against
  without circularity. Its two roles in the deliverables: the dashed reference
  lines (order of magnitude — deliberately not a fit target, per the calibration
  caveat we settled earlier), and the 1/p-scaling prediction (the 3.63 ratio
  between the two pT windows), which is the quantitative check the measurement
  passes at every split radius.

✻ Cogitated for 35s

❯ [Image #2] those two values are half max width of the two histogram?          
  ⎿  [Image #2]

● No — they are standard deviations (RMS values), not half-max widths. Each σ in
  the legend is the root-mean-square of that histogram's Δψ values — √⟨Δψ²⟩
  over all tracks (ROOT's GetRMS()), with the ± being its statistical error
  σ/√(2N). The "(core …)" number is the same RMS recomputed after iteratively
  discarding entries beyond 3σ — the width of the central Gaussian part with the
  rare hard-kink tails (≈2.5% of tracks) removed.

  The relation to the width you'd read off by eye: for a Gaussian, the full
  width at half maximum is FWHM = 2.355·σ (so the half-width at half max is
  ≈1.18·σ). But these distributions are visibly not Gaussian — they have heavier
  tails — and tails pull up the RMS while leaving the half-max width untouched.
  That means the width you see at half maximum tracks the core number, not the
  raw σ. You can check it on the plot: the green curve peaks near 0.29 and falls
  to half of that (≈0.145) at roughly ±0.35 mrad → FWHM ≈ 0.7 mrad → ÷2.355 ≈
  0.30 mrad = exactly the quoted green core. Same exercise on the blue curve
  gives ≈1.1–1.2, matching its core 1.17. The gap between raw σ and core (1.365
  vs 1.17, 0.367 vs 0.30) is precisely the tail contribution.

  Why the RMS is the quoted number rather than a half-max width: scattering
  theory's currency is the Gaussian-equivalent width — Highland's θ₀ is defined
  that way — and RMS widths combine in quadrature along the path and across
  noise sources, which half-max widths don't for non-Gaussian shapes. So σ is
  what's comparable to the Highland order-scale line and what feeds any
  resolution budget; the core-vs-raw pair is there to show you how much of σ is
  clean Gaussian scattering versus tail events.

❯ apply the same analysis to real data to check the mc mismatch there           

● Sharp extension — with one physics reality to design around: real data has no
  truth hits, so "the same analysis" must run on reconstructed clusters grouped 
  by the real tracker (ntp_clus_trk, grouped by seedID — the classical
  seed-island tracking output). And on clusters, the split-arc mismatch is
  resolution-dominated (~700 µm points over short levers ⇒ tens of mrad of fit
  noise vs the 1 mrad MS signal — which is precisely the professor's conclusion
  visualized). So the meaningful data-vs-MC check is: run the identical
  circle-fit + split machinery on real tracker-tracks and on sim truth-matched
  island clusters, selected identically by fitted curvature, and compare the
  resolution-driven widths. If the sim's cluster resolution is realistic, they
  should agree. Inspecting the real tree first:

● Done — figure clean (sim_validation_plots/ms_real_v53f.png, ledger
  island_post/ms_real_v53f.txt, producer island_post/ms_real.C). What "the same
  analysis" had to become on real data, and what it found:

  Setup. Real data has no truth hits, so the machinery runs on reconstructed
  clusters grouped into tracks: real side = ntp_clus_trk from the canonical
  run-79507 file, tracks = (event, seedID) groups from the classical seed-island
  tracking; sim side = island91 v5.3 truth-matched clusters, truth used only to
  group (playing the role seedID plays for real). Both sides then get identical
  treatment — same full-crosser gates, same circle fit, pT windows selected by
  fitted curvature on both sides (no truth in any selection), same split-arc
  tangent mismatch. Two forced adaptations, documented in the macro header: the
  cluster-level split runs at r₀ = 49, because pad rows begin at r = 31.4 cm and
  the 35 cm truth-hit border would leave ≤7 clusters inside — geometrically
  unusable; and the ±8 mrad core window is dropped, because at cluster level the
  mismatch is resolution noise ~20× larger than the scattering.

  Results:

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │                             │      pT ≈ 0.5 GeV      │  pT 1.5–2.5 GeV   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ tracks, real / sim          │ 694 / 12,568           │ 55 / 899          │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ circle-fit RMS median, real │ 692 µm                 │ 626 µm            │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ circle-fit RMS median, sim  │ 733 µm                 │ 469 µm            │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ data/MC                     │ 0.94                   │ 1.33              │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ σ(Δψ) at r₀=49, real (core) │ 25.7 ± 0.7 (17.9) mrad │ 31.2 ± 3.0 (16.8) │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ σ(Δψ) at r₀=49, sim (core)  │ 21.7 ± 0.1 (17.9) mrad │ 23.5 ± 0.6 (18.4) │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  Two conclusions.

  1. The real data demonstrates the professor's answer by itself. At cluster
  level, σ(Δψ) is ~17–31 mrad and essentially pT-independent in both real and
  sim — pure resolution noise, with no trace of the 1/p scattering scaling that
  the truth-hit analysis resolves at ~1 mrad. Multiple scattering is invisible
  under cluster resolution in the actual detector data, not just in argument.
  2. The MC mismatch itself is small at 0.5 GeV and interesting at high pT. At
  0.5 GeV the sim's cluster-level tracking resolution is realistic to 6% (692 vs
  733 µm, and the two distributions overlay almost bin-by-bin in the left
  panel; the split-mismatch cores agree to 0.3%, 17.88 vs 17.94 mrad — real's
  excess is all in tails, consistent with tracker mis-associations). At 1.5–2.5
  GeV, real is 33% worse than sim (626 vs 469 µm) — in quadrature that's ~415 µm
  of real-only scatter, and that is exactly the fingerprint of the alignment
  and static-distortion corrections the detached sim deliberately doesn't model
  (the long-declared dropped-payload item). It's masked at 0.5 GeV, where
  crossing-angle smearing dominates both sides, and exposed on stiff tracks,
  where the geometry gets quiet. Caveats before leaning on it: real high-pT
  statistics are thin (55 tracks, so ~3σ-level), and the real tracker curates
  its clusters (pruning outliers biases real widths low) while the sim side has
  no tracking inefficiency at all.

  That high-pT 1.33 ratio is a genuinely useful new observable for the pipeline
  thread — a per-track, distortion-sensitive data/MC meter that all the
  pixel-level validation is blind to. Per the scope rule I haven't written it
  into their ledger; it's in ms_real_v53f.txt if you want to relay it.

✻ Worked for 6m 3s

❯ since we confirmed a real track should be able to fit to a circle, can we fit 
  in the real data, although a hard work, to double check if ntp_clus_trk is    
  really true track, then redo the circle fitting after the check?              

● Yes — and it's a genuinely good use of the circularity result: since we've
  established that a true track must fit a circle at the ~0.7 mm
  cluster-resolution level, circularity becomes an independent audit of the real
  tracker's associations — fakes and mis-attached clusters can't hide from it.
  The plan: fit every (event, seedID) group (not just full crossers),
  iteratively remove outlier clusters (|residual| > 3 mm, ≈4σ of resolution),
  classify each track as CLEAN / RESCUED (real track carrying mis-associated
  clusters) / FAKE-like (no consistent circle), audit the transverse impact
  parameter as a physics sanity check, then redo the circle-fit comparison on 
  the purified sample — with the identical cleaning applied to the sim side so
  the data/MC redo stays apples-to-apples. Not so hard at this scale (6,138 real
  groups). Adding the check to ms_real.C:
